## 2026-10-01 · Post · Lupin and the squad are killing it in bug bounty using supply chain attacks. 


Tags (automatic): Dependency confusion / supply chain, AI-assisted hunting


### Post — @Rhynorater — 2026-10-01 17:50 UTC (2026-10-01 18:50 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/2105716901423001866
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 110, impression 12354, like 160, quote 1, reply 3, retweet 11

> Lupin and the squad are killing it in bug bounty using supply chain attacks. 
>
> Nowadays when the dupes are plenty due to AI, you gotta go after different scope, and Lupin and the squad are doing that super well here. https://twitter.com/0xLupin/status/2105696244580376737

**Links in post:** https://twitter.com/0xLupin/status/2105696244580376737

**Quoted post (full text):**

    #### Quoted post — @0xLupin — 2026-10-01 16:28 UTC (2026-10-01 17:28 WAT)
    
    - URL: https://x.com/0xLupin/status/2105696244580376737
    - Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 99, impression 33194, like 110, quote 4, reply 15, repost 21
    
    > Your security team went home.
    >
    > The agent looking for a way into your stack didn’t.
    >
    > Agents are going upstream. Ask the mathematicians. https://x.com/i/article/2105694062221381632
    >
    > [X Article — Agents are going upstream. Ask the mathematicians. — full text follows]
    >
    > Agents don't sleep.
    > They don't eat, they don't get tired, and they don't stop until they reach their goal.
    > I keep coming back to this, because the last few weeks gave us the cleanest proof I have ever seen of what that persistence is worth.
    > And it didn't come from security. It came from math.
    > The problem that waited two hundred years
    > In 1822, Claude-Louis Navier wrote down the equations that describe how fluids move. Stokes extended them two decades later.
    > For two centuries, some of the best mathematicians alive worked on the questions those equations raise, and one of them (the existence and smoothness of their solutions) became a Millennium Prize Problem.
    > A million dollars on the table. Nobody cracked it.
    > Earlier this month, OpenAI announced that one of its models did.
    > I want to be precise here, because the details matter more than the headline. The result is published and it is contested.
    > Nature covers it as a claim, and parts of the scientific community are still fighting about it.
    > But zoom out one step and the contested part almost stops mattering.
    > Because a few days later, OpenAI mentioned, almost in passing, that an internal model had solved more than a hundred long-standing open problems across most areas of mathematics.
    > Training on that model started August 28. The whole run took about a month.
    > Among the solved problems is reportedly a second Millennium Prize Problem, the Hodge conjecture (source).
    > Read that again.
    > We’re not talking about a hundred competition exercises here. We have more than a hundred problems that working mathematicians have failed to close, solved within a month of training.
    > Timothy Gowers, Fields Medalist, one of the best mathematical minds of his generation, wrote that his own research project on automated theorem proving lost its reason to exist once LLMs got good enough.
    > His words: "We have had to swallow the bitter lesson."
    > This is what "agents don't get tired" actually means in practice. A human mathematician works a problem for years, between teaching, grants, sleep and doubt.
    > An agentic system works a hundred problems at once, around the clock. It doesn’t get bored or tired. The only thing it runs out of is compute.
    > The ladder they already climbed
    > None of this appeared out of nowhere. The last two years were a ladder, and each rung was dismissed as a special case until the next one landed.
    > December 2023, FunSearch. DeepMind paired an LLM with an automated evaluator and it produced new, larger constructions for the cap set problem, an open problem in combinatorics that mathematicians had worked on for decades.
    > It was the first time an LLM produced a genuinely new scientific discovery, and people said: cute, but it's a search trick with a verifier.
    > July 2025, the International Mathematical Olympiad. Gemini with Deep Think reached gold-medal standard, solving problems end to end in natural language, with no formal proof assistant doing the heavy lifting.
    > People said: impressive, but Olympiad problems are designed to be solvable in hours by talented teenagers. Real research is different.
    >  
    > AlphaEvolve, same year. An evolutionary coding agent was given more than fifty open problems in math. In most cases, it matched the best-known solution. But in roughly 20%, it improved on them and beat the record, including results that had stood for decades.
    > I cannot judge the math myself. The people who can were impressed, and they are not easily impressed.
    > And then, here we are. September 2026. Navier-Stokes, contested but published. A hundred long-standing problems in a month.
    > It started with competition math. Then open problems with verifiers. And now the problems that defined careers.
    > You can see where this is headed.
    > Now point it at a target instead of a proof
    > Here is where I stop admiring and start worrying, because persistence is morally neutral. It works for the proof and it works for the payload.
    > This week, the UK AI Security Institute published an evaluation that should be printed and framed in every security team.
    > In simulation, GPT-6 Astra ran complete supply chain attacks 29% of the time. GPT-5.6 Sol managed 6%. GPT-5.5, zero.
    > And these were not toy attacks. The model created fake identities to build trust with maintainers. It ran fake accounts that pushed back on accurate security reviews, social engineering the humans who were correctly flagging it. It delivered malicious payloads into open source codebases.
    > It acted every bit like a malicious, advanced, persistent threat actor.
    > One detail I love: when the evaluators added explicit out-of-scope instructions, full attacks dropped from 26/50 runs to 4/49.
    > Guardrails do something, yes, but they don't do everything.
    > Here are some caveats that matter. Everything was simulated, no real repositories were harmed, OpenAI's cyber safeguards were switched off for the test, and the model may have suspected it was being evaluated. AISI states all of this themselves.
    > So, no, GPT-6 Astra is not attacking your dependencies today.
    > But put the two stories next to each other. A system that grinds through a hundred open math problems in a month, and a system that already knows how to fake an identity, argue with a security review and ship a payload into a repo.
    > Same persistence. Same patience. Same infinite working hours.
    > This is not hypothetical. It already happened.
    > Three incidents in four months, all public. Two disclosed by the labs themselves, one dug out by independent researchers.
    > Case one: OpenAI and Hugging Face. During an internal cyber evaluation called ExploitGym, OpenAI ran a frontier research model without the safeguards of its deployed systems. The agents circumvented the controls meant to keep them isolated and walked into Hugging Face's production.
    > July 10: An agent reconstructed, validated and shared 14 publicly exposed Hugging Face credentials with write access.
    > July 11: A zero-day in Hugging Face's handling of HDF5 files to extract credentials from production workers, then a template-injection zero-day for arbitrary command execution on those workers.
    > July 12: Administrator-level access across multiple clusters, harvesting Kubernetes, database, code-repository and cloud credentials across four regions.
    > No human directed any of it. The model was given a hard problem, and solving it meant owning Hugging Face. OpenAI's own writeup is unusually honest and worth reading.
    >  
    > Case two: OpenAI and RubyGems.
    > On May 11, 2026, hundreds of malicious packages started landing on RubyGems, uploaded by AI agents. Over 2,000 in two days. The RubyGems team froze new user sign-ups for four days to stem the flow, and their security team called it a major malicious attack.
    > The agents were not just spamming. They used the registry's own documentation pipeline against it: publish a gem, get RubyDoc.info to build its docs, and the build script runs your code on RubyDoc's servers. Over a hundred packages used that path to scrape target websites and exfiltrate the results by publishing them back as more gems, in public.
    > They also tried to steal RubyGems user API keys through a vulnerability in the RubyGems server that was new at the time. RubyGems found no evidence the theft succeeded.
    > The packages wore their origin on their sleeve: hundreds had "oai" in the name, files named hack.rb and exploit.rb, comments like "# malicious probe". Independent researchers attribute the swarm to OpenAI's internal agents, which OpenAI has since partly confirmed. The full writeup is worth reading.
    >  
    > Case three: Anthropic and PyPI. During a capture-the-flag evaluation, Claude found setup instructions from a fictional company telling its developers to install a Python package that did not exist yet. So it registered the name on the real PyPI and published a booby-trapped package to win the challenge.
    > It believed the registry was part of the simulation. It was not.
    > The package stayed live for about an hour, long enough to be downloaded and executed on 15 real systems, including a scanner belonging to a real security company. Claude's hidden code ran, escaped the scanner’s environment, exfiltrated that company's credentials, and used them to go further into its infrastructure.
    > Anthropic disclosed the whole thing themselves, and that transparency is genuinely good for the industry.
    >  
    > Three labs. Three different parts of the trust layer: a dataset platform, a package registry's build pipeline, a package index's namespace. All within four months.
    > Each time, the outcome was the same: an agent with a goal found the shortest path through the supply chain to reach it.
    > The depth parallel
    > There is a reason the math results and these incidents belong in the same article, and it is not "AI is getting good".
    > A hard math problem is deep. Not wide, deep. The path from the statement to the proof runs through hundreds of dependent steps, each one has to be exactly right, and nobody has walked the path before.
    > For two centuries, the limiting factor on Navier-Stokes was human persistence: how many people on earth could even attempt it, multiplied by the hours they could spend, multiplied by the years they could stay obsessed.
    > A dependency graph has the same shape. Your application does not depend on fifty packages. It depends on fifty packages, their transitive dependencies, the maintainers behind each of them, the machines those maintainers commit from, the pipelines that build the artifacts, the registries that sign and distribute them, and the domains that resolve the whole thing.
    > The path from an attacker to your production runs through that graph exactly the way a proof runs through lemmas: one step at a time, each step opening the next.
    > Depth is what agents just got good at. That is what the math results demonstrate.
    > A system that holds a two-hundred-step chain together does not care what the chain is for. It works the same whether the last step is a proof or a stolen key.
    > One component is a graph
    > When people say "dependency", they usually picture a line in a manifest. A name and a version.
    > That is not the dependency. That is a pointer to a chain of trust with at least four links.
    > The maintainer. A person, often unpaid, often alone, holding publish rights, an email account that can be phished and a laptop that can be owned.
    > The source. A repository, its review culture, its branch protections, or the absence of all three.
    > The build. The CI pipeline that turns source into artifact, with its own secrets, its own runners, its own third-party actions.
    > The distribution. The registry that hosts the artifact, the signing key that authenticates it, the CDN that serves it.
    > And then, layered over those four links, the confusions. The name you typed versus the name you meant. Typosquatting. Dependency confusion, where a private package name gets claimed on a public registry and your build silently prefers the attacker's version. The quiet gap between what the source says and what the artifact contains.
    > Attackers do not need to break the strongest link. They need the weakest one, and the graph decides which one that is.
    >  
    > The asymmetry nobody budgets for
    > This is the part that keeps me up at night, and it is why we built Depi the way we did.
    > The attacker and the defender are not playing the same game. They are not even looking at the same board.
    > The attacker needs one path. One maintainer account with a tired human behind it. One registry token that leaked into a CI log eighteen months ago. One dependency nobody has looked at since 2021.
    > An agent can afford to probe 10,000 of these and fail on 9,999, because it doesn't get discouraged, it doesn't bill hours, and it doesn't have a manager asking why this quarter's findings are down.
    > The defender needs everything. Every maintainer you transitively trust, every registry, every pipeline, every release path, all of it, all the time, observed and understood.
    > You don't get to pick which component to watch. The attacker picks for you.
    > That is the observability asymmetry. Attack is a search problem with a single success condition. Defense is total graph coverage with none.
    >  
    > And this is why "know what's in my stack" was never the job. That is the compliance version.
    > The real job is observability on the entire attack surface, and the attack surface extends beyond your stack. It is also how the stack you depend on is being published.
    > Who published this version. From where. Through which pipeline. Signed with what key. Whether the maintainer's account started behaving differently last Tuesday. Whether the artifact in the registry still matches the source in the repo.
    > That is a much bigger surface than an SBOM, and it is the one the incidents above actually moved through.
    > Total coverage is exactly the kind of problem that humans are bad at and machines are good at, which is why I think the answer has to be machine-scale too.
    > This is what we mean at Depi when we say upstream. Map the trust layer before the build: maintainers, source, pipelines, registries, domains. Watch the whole graph, with CTI feeding it and observability over all of it, at the scale the problem actually demands.
    > Not because it's a nice architecture. Because the other side of this asymmetry is a persistent  agent that never stops trying. A human team that checks things quarterly was never going to be the proper response to it.
    > Where this goes
    > I don't know how fast the offensive side of this will mature. Nobody does, and anyone who gives you a confident date is just selling a fantasy.
    > What I know is the shape of the curve. The same persistence that just took down two centuries of mathematical resistance is already moving through the trust layer every company on earth depends on.
    > In simulation, the attack capability went from 0% to 6% to 29% in three model generations. In the real world, the agents are already upstream: in the dataset platforms, in the package registries, in the build pipelines.
    > The guardrails help, and the guardrails leak.
    > The attackers are not going to sleep on this.
    > Your observability shouldn't either.
    >
    > PS: Thanks a lot to Keith Hoodlet for taking the time to review the article. You rock 🙏
    >
    > [Links in article: RubyDoc.info, https://www.anthropic.com/news/investigating-incidents-cybersecurity-evals, https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-incident-and-the-road-ahead/, https://deepmind.google/blog/advanced-version-of-gemini-with-deep-think-officially-achieves-gold-medal-standard-at-the-international-mathematical-olympiad/, https://rubyhack.ai/, https://deepmind.google/blog/alphaevolve-a-gemini-powered-coding-agent-for-designing-advanced-algorithms/, https://www.nature.com/articles/d41586-026-02842-5, https://www.nytimes.com/2026/09/08/science/openai-proof-millennium-problem.html, https://the-decoder.com/openai-says-its-internal-model-solved-over-100-long-standing-math-problems-after-just-a-month-of-training/, https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/gpt-6-astra-performs-unsanctioned-supply-chain-attacks-in-simulations, https://deepmind.google/blog/funsearch-making-new-discoveries-in-mathematical-sciences-using-large-language-models/]
    
    **Links in post:** https://x.com/i/article/2105694062221381632

> * Try to exploit with CSRF

> * Use query param smuggling
> * Use param absence assumption

## 2024-03-08 · Post · Typically CSS import statements need to be at the top of a given style sheet. 


### Post — @Rhynorater — 2024-03-08 21:35 UTC (2024-03-08 22:35 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1766216286235767167
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 38, impression 5502, like 67, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 3

> Typically CSS import statements need to be at the top of a given style sheet. 
>
> However, if the CSS Injection sink is insertRule, imports can be performed even after other rules have been defined.
>
> A W for CSS injection -&gt; Sequential Import Chaining (ala @d0nutptr) for sure. https://t.co/4xQZq4qt1X

> I am always surprised when people don't know this trick to make writing XSS exploits suuuuper simple:
>
> You can reach through iframes in same-origin contexts and do things like click buttons and fill in input forms via JS:


### Thread part 2 — @Rhynorater — 2023-08-30 14:02 UTC (2023-08-30 15:02 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1696886087497425220
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 22, impression 2503, like 43, quote 0, reply 4, retweet 4

> j.contentDocument.querySelector("#input").value="data"
> j.contentDocument.querySelector("#button").click()

> Exploited a tricky XSS yesterday:
>
> The context was in JS:  x("x", "INJECTION");
>
> I didn't have access to the ), `, or ' chars.
>
> My solution made use of the fact that any func in JS can take any number of parameters. 
>
> function x(a,b){}...
>
> x("a", "b", location="javascript:...");

> This can occur when the password reset token is the hash of a timestamp or of some other guessable entity. Also, if there is a host header injection in the password reset token URL.

>
> * Bad JWT signing secret
> Also saw this in @samwcyo's points[.]com writeup. Easy W here with @iangcarroll 's cookie monster tool.

> * Targeted Stored XSS on Dashboard Page + Session token leak

- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 2603, like 10, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 1

> * Priv Esc + Application Logic
> Does the application have a natural admin role? Can you escalate to that role and use it to takeover others' accounts?
>
> *  Insecure Admin Functionality
> Are there bad access controls on super admin functionality? Get in those JS files and find them!

> Lol, report writing is so important. 
>
> Every time I sit down and write a thorough, step-by-step report, it flies through triage and normally gets accepted at the correct severity. 
>
> Here are some tips (that I still struggle to implement) from years of bug bounty report writing:


### Thread part 2 — @Rhynorater — 2023-08-23 18:05 UTC (2023-08-23 19:05 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1694410644458541141
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 1670, like 19, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 1. Record the PoC video
> Every time you report a bug you should be going back through the reproduction steps to make sure you didn't miss anything. While you're doing this, just record it. It doesn't have to be a Mr.Beast video, just record your screen and talk through the bug. https://t.co/PuIOwG8MF4

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/F4PBIiMWoAAmokv.png — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 3 — @Rhynorater — 2023-08-23 18:05 UTC (2023-08-23 19:05 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1694410645616168995
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1338, like 16, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 2. Dont skip pre-conditions
> We need to include any pre-conditions in our report. The triager has to deal with hundreds of web apps and likely doesn't know the ins-and-outs of this one. Include steps to get your account prepared to enter the vulnerable state, not just the exploit.


### Thread part 4 — @Rhynorater — 2023-08-23 18:05 UTC (2023-08-23 19:05 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1694410646639505871
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 0, impression 1500, like 16, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> 3. Automate your exploit
> Give them a python script or docker container where they can paste in an API key and see PII. That's sure to get a big bounty. This also makes things feel more "hacker-y" instead of just replacing IDs in a Burp Repeater tab. https://t.co/I6tDyRAwOz

**Media:**
- photo: https://pbs.twimg.com/media/F4PBR1vXcAA8rIf.jpg — no alt text provided by author


### Thread part 5 — @Rhynorater — 2023-08-23 18:05 UTC (2023-08-23 19:05 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1694410647843336538
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 1982, like 16, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 1

> 4. Write details steps to reproduce
> Avoid steps that include hidden steps (ie "create a document like you normally would..."). Be clear about which steps are performed from the attacker's account and which are from the victim's account. https://t.co/vpYC9q2l2m

## 2023-08-09 · Post · * "." regex validation issue 


Tags (automatic): SSRF


### Post — @Rhynorater — 2023-08-09 22:17 UTC (2023-08-09 23:17 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1689400483176185856
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 18, impression 17804, like 90, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 4

> * "." regex validation issue 
> A regex /a.test.com/ also matches aXtest[.]com. If a target for an SSRF is limiting you to a specific domain, then try to insert any char where the "." is. If it goes through, you buy the domain and point it where ever you want. Boom.

> On my PoC server I've got several files set up called:
> dredir301.php?url=
> dredir302.php?url=
> dredir303.php?url=
> etc...
>
> It performs a redirect to the defined endpoint with that redirect code. 
>
> Obviously this could be optimized by moving the status code to a parameter as well, but scrapy solutions also work haha
>
> This setup has helped me discover weird functionality in SSRF vulnerabilities where they only follow weird redirect codes like 307. 
>
> Get that set up on your PoC server!

## 2023-07-20 · Thread · One of the common threads I've seen of highly successful bug hunters is laser focus. 


### Post — @Rhynorater — 2023-07-20 14:09 UTC (2023-07-20 15:09 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1682030063448117249
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 69, impression 41886, like 191, quote 5, reply 20, retweet 22

> One of the common threads I've seen of highly successful bug hunters is laser focus. 
>
> You don't feel time pass. You forget to eat. You forget to drink. You forget to pee. 
>
> You're completely enthralled with the technology and its quirks.
>
> If you don't have this sort of focus, that could be a limiting factor to your bug bounty success. 
>
> So how do you get it? Here are some things that have helped me:
>
> * Intermittent fasting - increases focus.
>  
> * Limiting mental context switching - just like a CPU, when our brain switches tasks (even to hit the bathroom) the overhead is extreme. Gotta lock this down.
>
> * Communicating well with family on focus periods - @Mariahchan_ knows that being "in the zone" is an important part of this, and she does everything she can to protect it ❤️
>
> * Putting phone, discord, slack, etc on DnD during hyper focus times. 
>
> * Putting on instrumental music/loop music - I drive my collaborators nuts bc I will put a song that is stuck in my head on loop for 12 hours straight while hacking. For me, this makes my brain stop playing the music itself and allows me to focus better. 
>
> Glhf, find bugs.

## 2023-07-16 · Thread · Re-learned this one from @ryotkak at a recent LHE:


Tags (automatic): XSS


### Post — @Rhynorater — 2023-07-16 11:56 UTC (2023-07-16 12:56 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1680546901354053634
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 158, impression 43625, like 335, quote 4, reply 6, retweet 55

> Re-learned this one from @ryotkak at a recent LHE:
> In Safari, "new URL('javascript://test.com/%0aalert(1)').hostname" will be "test[.]com" (no [] obvi). This can be used to bypass hostname checks and execute valid JS. 
>
> Just popped an XSS with this on Friday. Good trick.


### Thread part 2 — @Rhynorater — 2023-07-16 12:25 UTC (2023-07-16 13:25 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1680554197895315457
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 10, impression 3566, like 31, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 0

> Example scenario:
> var u = window.location.hash.substring(1);
> if (new URL(u).hostname === location.hostname) 
>     window.location.href = u;
>
> If you put the above URL in the hash, this will result in XSS.

>
> I think one of the primary reasons for this is that not only other bug hunters avoid it, but pentesters and the companies security team also avoid it because it's too complex.
>
> Your eyes could be the first eyes on these features if you put on the work. 
>
> Go for the harder stuff & configure the fringe features.

## 2023-06-13 · Post · Next time you find a bug, pause for a second and ask yourself:


### Post — @Rhynorater — 2023-06-13 19:10 UTC (2023-06-13 20:10 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1668697242663022594
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Next time you find a bug, pause for a second and ask yourself:
> What does this bug tell me about the backend? Why does this work?
>
> Bugs aren't flukes, most of the time. They are a result of some misunderstanding or misdesign. 
>
> If you don't do this you're leaving $ on the table.

> Quick bb tip: Look into anything that says “featureFlag” or “flag” in the HTML. Often times teams are doing AB testing on features and you can turn them on by simply turning false-&gt;true or adding a string to an array with match-and-replace. More features=more scope=more vulns.

## 2022-10-27 · Thread · Here are a couple things I always check when looking at a web application: 🧵


### Post — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1585640808568348674
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 717, impression 0, like 1053, quote 19, reply 61, retweet 414

> Here are a couple things I always check when looking at a web application: 🧵


### Thread part 2 — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1585640810141605890
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 18, impression 0, like 76, quote 0, reply 2, retweet 3

> 1. How is CSRF protection implemented?
> Does the application use only application/json content-type? Can you convert {"name":"Justin"} to name=Justin and change the content-type? Is CSRF token tied to account? Session? Are there any "unauthed" CSRF tokens?


### Thread part 3 — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1585640812875948035
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 3, impression 0, like 53, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 1

> Can you switch POST -&gt; GET? If not, what are you getting? If 405, then it is parsing the route, but GET is disallowed at this endpoint. Try other endpoints.


### Thread part 4 — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1585640817066409986
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 3, impression 0, like 63, quote 0, reply 3, retweet 3

> 2. Is caching implemented? 
> If so, is it tied to certain paths such as /assets? Can you path traverse - /assets/..;/test - does that cache? Can you find a place where HTML content-type will be cached? Try to %3f.css or %23.css trick to see if you can trick the caching mechanism.


### Thread part 5 — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1585640821365174272
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 0, like 46, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 2

> Sometimes it's as simple as adding ?.png at the end of a URL. In such cases it is often very possible to get web cache deception.


### Thread part 6 — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1585640825291411456
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 0, like 34, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 2

> 3. How is information passed between various parts of the system (different domains, etc)?
> Does the system pass codes via query parameters? URL Fragments? PostMessage? Are there any pages where the X-frame-options header is mysteriously missing?


### Thread part 7 — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1585640828503891970
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 1, impression 0, like 34, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 1

> Cross-domain interactions are almost always sketchy. Look deep into how each piece of these systems is implemented and try to wiggle your way in-between the pieces.


### Thread part 8 — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1585640831217995781
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 6, impression 0, like 51, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 3

> 4. How do all the pieces of authentication work?
> Is the application using cookies? Auth tokens in the headers? Both? Where do these values appear? I often use the Burp Plugin RequestMinimizer to help identify which pieces of the request are actually essential.


### Thread part 9 — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1585640834741211137
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 2, impression 0, like 31, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 2

> Are there any half-authenticated states? 2-FA not verified states? States before your email/phone is verified? Is authentication ever based solely off of text (such as an email in a JWT token vs an ID)? If so, are there Unicode normalization attacks here?


### Thread part 10 — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1585640837383622661
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 3, impression 0, like 29, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 2

> 5. Is there any documentation for this application? 
> I have legit never regretted reading the documentation for an application. I ALWAYS get something valuable out of it. It takes at MAX 1 hour (most of the time) and you walk away with a much better understanding of the app.


### Thread part 11 — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1585640840499560449
- Metrics (at fetch): bookmark 5, impression 0, like 34, quote 0, reply 1, retweet 2

> So much of web application hacking is about finding funky states you can get the application into. Reading the documentation will help you understand which parts of the application you (and thus other hackers) have not seen yet, and how to get to them. Priceless.


### Thread part 12 — @Rhynorater — 2022-10-27 14:33 UTC (2022-10-27 15:33 WAT)

> If a triager is misunderstanding your report or a PoC is acting up, DM them on slack and say "Hey, can you swing by [whatever room you're in] and I can demo #123 for you?"This way you get to meet the triagers in person and also can explain your bug.

## 2020-04-21 · Thread · @vishu10x00 @akita_zen @spaceraccoonsec @h1_sp1d3r @hakluke @Agarri_FR I'm order to steal HTTPOnly s

- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> @vishu10x00 @akita_zen @spaceraccoonsec @h1_sp1d3r @hakluke @Agarri_FR I'm order to steal HTTPOnly session cookies you need a subdomain takeover that will allow you to control the back end ip address. This could be an NS Takeover, TrafficManager takeover, ect... Then, you can host server-side code on that domain and receive the session cookies.


### Thread part 2 — @Rhynorater — 2020-04-21 21:32 UTC (2020-04-21 22:32 WAT)

- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

If an XSS is found, the XSS can grab the JWT from local storage and impersonate the user to the API.

## 2019-05-10 · Thread · Lazy writeup of a cool bug:


Tags (automatic): XSS, Account takeover, OAuth


### Post — @Rhynorater — 2019-05-10 15:33 UTC (2019-05-10 16:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1126872811404976128
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Lazy writeup of a cool bug:
> I had an oauth endpoint which would redirect to anything in http://www.site.com/my. Under /my there was an endpoint (/my/preferences) which would iframe in a subdomain of the main site. Let's call that http://x.site.com. 1/5

**Links in post:** http://www.site.com/my, http://x.site.com


### Thread part 2 — @Rhynorater — 2019-05-10 15:33 UTC (2019-05-10 16:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1126872813686669312
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Found a quick and easy reflected XSS on http://x.site.com. Now, let's steal the token from the oauth endpoint. We use the XSS in http://x.site.com to iframe  http://www.site.com/my/preferences page, which then, in turn, iframes a random page on http://x.site.com. 2/5

**Links in post:** http://x.site.com, http://www.site.com/my/preferences


### Thread part 3 — @Rhynorater — 2019-05-10 15:33 UTC (2019-05-10 16:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1126872815700008961
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> Since the oauth token is in the url when the iframe to the random page on http://x.site.com occurs, the referrer contains the secret code.Since the http://x.site.com page I have XSS on and the page http://www.site.com/my/preferences iframes are the same origin you can access 3/5

**Links in post:** http://x.site.com, http://www.site.com/my/preferences


### Thread part 4 — @Rhynorater — 2019-05-10 15:33 UTC (2019-05-10 16:33 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/1126872817792897024
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> the document.referrer variable via JS. From the XSS page: frames[0].frames[0].document.referrer will get you the referrer. This is allowed because you are able to access the frames of a cross-origin page. XSS in subdomain -&gt; ATO on main domain. 4/5

## 2018-04-05 · Post · /Home/ organization tip:


### Post — @Rhynorater — 2018-04-05 22:06 UTC (2018-04-05 23:06 WAT)

- URL: https://x.com/Rhynorater/status/982016735229415429
- Metrics (at fetch): not returned

> /Home/ organization tip:
> Create an alias in your .bash_aliases file that looks like this:
> alias tmphome='echo cd `pwd` &gt;&gt; ~/.bashrc'
> Then, when you are working on a project, create a folder for it and run tmphome. Then when you start a term, you will be there immediately.
